======================================================================
ARTICLE: Miniseries
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{Short description|Television show with few episodes}}
{{about|a TV show or series that has a predetermined number of episodes|the album by Sumin & Slom|Miniseries (album)}}
{{More citations needed|date=March 2026}}
{{Use dmy dates|date=September 2026}}

A '''miniseries''' or '''mini-series''', sometimes called a '''limited-run series''', is a [[television program]] that tells a story in a predetermined, limited number of episodes (generally 2–12).<ref>{{cite web|last1=Mitra |first1=Mallika |title=Miniseries may be the competitive edge streamers are looking for |url=https://www.cnbc.com/2019/07/24/miniseries-may-be-the-competitive-edge-streamers-are-looking-for.html |website=[[CNBC]] |date=24 July 2019}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|title=Miniseries – Definition, Usage & Quiz |url=https://ultimatelexicon.com/definitions/m/miniseries/ |website=Ultimate Lexicon |date=17 September 2024 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20251211185853/https://ultimatelexicon.com/definitions/m/miniseries/ |archive-date=11 December 2025 |url-status=dead}}</ref> Many miniseries can also be referred to, and shown, as a [[television film]] in several parts.

The term "[[Serial (radio and television)|serial]]" is used in the United Kingdom and in other [[Commonwealth of Nations|Commonwealth]] nations to describe a show that has an ongoing narrative plotline, while "series" is used for a set of episodes in a similar way that "season" is used in North America.

==Definitions==
A miniseries is distinguished from an ongoing television series; the latter does not usually have a predetermined number of episodes and may continue for several years. Before the term was coined in the United States in the early 1970s, the ongoing episodic form was always called a "[[Serial (radio and television)|serial]]", just as a [[novel]] appearing in episodes in successive editions of magazines or newspapers is called a [[Serial (literature)|serial]]. In Britain, miniseries are often still referred to as serials or series.

Several commentators have offered more precise definitions of the term. In ''Halliwell's Television Companion'' (1987), [[Leslie Halliwell]] and [[Philip Purser]] suggest that miniseries tend to "appear in four to six episodes of various lengths",<ref name="MBC">{{cite web |url=http://www.museum.tv/eotv/miniseries.htm |publisher=Museum of Broadcast Communication |title=Miniseries| archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150707221948/http://www.museum.tv/eotv/miniseries.htm| archive-date=7 July 2015 |access-date=9 March 2015}}</ref><ref>[[Leslie Halliwell]], and [[Philip Purser]], ''Halliwell's Television Companion'', London: Paladin, 1987</ref> while Stuart Cunningham in ''Textual Innovation in the Australian Historical Mini-series'' (1989) defined a miniseries as "a limited run program of more than two and less than the 13-part season or half season block associated with serial or series programming".<ref name="MBC" /><ref>Cunningham, Stuart. "Textual Innovation in the Australian Historical Mini-series", chapter in ''Australian Television: Programs, Pleasures and Politics'', Sidney: Allen and Unwin, 1989</ref> With the proliferation of the format in the 1980s and 1990s, television films broadcast over even two or three nights were commonly referred to as miniseries in the United States.<ref name="NYT Sins">{{cite news| url=https://www.nytimes.com/1986/01/31/arts/joan-collins-in-sins-a-mini-series.html|work=[[The New York Times]]|first=John|last=Corry|title=Joan Collins in ''Sins'', a Mini-Series|date=31 January 1986 |access-date=7 March 2015}}</ref><ref name="Variety Jewels">{{cite web |url=https://variety.com/1992/tv/reviews/nbc-movie-of-the-week-danielle-steel-s-jewels-1200430797/ |title=Review: NBC Movie of the Week Danielle Steel's ''Jewels'' |work=[[Variety (magazine)|Variety]] |first=Tony |last=Scott |date=16 October 1992 |access-date=8 March 2015}}</ref>

In ''Television: A History'' (1985), [[Francis Wheen]] points out a difference in character development between the two: "Both soap operas and primetime series cannot afford to allow their leading characters to develop, since the shows are made with the intention of running indefinitely. In a miniseries on the other hand, there is a clearly defined beginning, middle, and end (as in a conventional play or novel), enabling characters to change, mature, or die as the serial proceeds".<ref name="MBC" /><ref name="Wheen">[[Francis Wheen]]; ''Television: A History'', London: Century Publishing, 1985</ref>

In 2015, the [[Academy of Television Arts & Sciences]] changed its guidelines on how [[Emmy]] nominees are classified, with shows with a limited run all referred to as "limited series" instead of "miniseries". This was a reversion to 1974, when the category was named "outstanding limited series". It had been changed to "outstanding miniseries" in 1986. Miniseries were put in the same category as [[made-for-television]] films from 2011 to 2014, before being given separate categories again.<ref>{{cite web | last=Albiniak | first=Paige | title=Rule Changes Open Gates to More Hopefuls | website=[[Broadcasting & Cable]] | date=25 May 2015 | url=https://www.nexttv.com/news/rule-changes-open-gates-more-hopefuls-141149 | access-date=25 October 2021}}</ref>

===21st-century definitions===
The ''[[Collins English Dictionary]]'' defines a miniseries as "a television programme in several parts that is shown on consecutive days or weeks for a short period; while ''[[Webster's New World College Dictionary]]'''s (4th ed., 2010, US) definition is "a TV drama or docudrama broadcast serially in a limited number of episodes".<ref name="ced">{{cite web | title=Miniseries definition and meaning | website=[[Collins English Dictionary]] | url=https://www.collinsdictionary.com/dictionary/english/miniseries | access-date=25 October 2021}}</ref>

In popular usage, by around 2020, the boundaries between miniseries and limited series have become somewhat blurred; the format has been described as a series with "a self-contained narrative – whether three or 12 episodes long".<ref name="Davies 2020">{{cite web | last=Davies | first=Hannah J. | title=Less! Less! Less!: How the miniseries took over TV | website=[[The Guardian]] | date=2 June 2020 | url=http://www.theguardian.com/tv-and-radio/2020/jun/02/less-less-less-how-the-miniseries-took-over-tv | access-date=25 October 2021}}</ref>

==History==
===United Kingdom===
The British television "serial" is rooted in dramatic radio productions developed between the [[World War I|first]] and [[World War II|second]] World Wars. In the 1920s, the [[BBC]] pioneered dramatic readings of books. In 1925, it broadcast ''[[A Christmas Carol]]'', which became a holiday favourite. Later, [[John Reith, 1st Baron Reith|John Reith]], wanting to use radio waves to "part the clouds of ignorance", came up with the idea for ''[[Classic Serial]]'', based on a "classical" literary text.{{sfnp|Giddings|Selby|2001|p=1}}

In 1939, the BBC adapted the romantic novel ''[[The Prisoner of Zenda]]'' for radio broadcast. Its adapter, Jack Inglis, compressed several characters into one and simplified the plotline. The production struck a chord with listeners and served as a prototype for serials that followed it.{{sfnp|Giddings|Selby|2001|pp=10–11}}

Post-war, [[BBC Television]] picked up the classic radio serial tradition by broadcasting ''[[The Warden (TV series)|The Warden]]'' by [[Anthony Trollope]] over six episodes in 1951. ''[[Pride and Prejudice]]'' was serialised in 1952, ''[[Jane Eyre]]'' in 1955. In 1953, the BBC broadcast the first serial written specifically for television: the six-part ''[[The Quatermass Experiment]]''. Its success paved the way for two more six-part serials: ''Quatermass II'' in 1955 and ''Quatermass and the Pit'' in 1958. In November 1960, the BBC televised a 13-episode adaptation of [[Charles Dickens]]'s ''[[Barnaby Rudge (TV series)|Barnaby Rudge]]''. In December of that year, it broadcast a four-episode dramatisation of [[Jane Austen]]'s ''[[Persuasion (novel)|Persuasion]]''.{{sfnp|Giddings|Selby|2001|p=20}}

To compete with commercial television, BBC launched [[BBC2]] in 1964. It had a new time slot allocated for classic serial adaptations on Saturday evenings. The late-night broadcast allowed for more risky and sophisticated choices and for longer episodes. In 1967, ''[[The Forsyte Saga (1967 TV series)|The Forsyte Saga]]'' was broadcast in 26 50-minute episodes. Following its success in Britain, the series was shown in the United States on public television and broadcast all over the world and became the first BBC television series to be sold to the [[Soviet Union]].<ref name="pbs">''{{Deprecated archive|url=https://archive.today/20130415145017/http://www.pbs.org/wgbh/masterpiece/forsyte/ei_saga.html|title=The Forsyte Saga}}'' at PBS [[Masterpiece Theatre]], URL accessed 12 October 2009</ref><ref name="televisionheaven">{{cite web |url=http://www.televisionheaven.co.uk/forsyte.htm |title=The Forsyte Saga |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090709113212/http://www.televisionheaven.co.uk/forsyte.htm |archive-date=9 July 2009 |work=televisionheaven.co.uk}}</ref>

===North America===
[[Anthology series]] dominated American dramatic programming during the [[Golden Age of Television]], when "every night was opening night; one never knew when a flick of the knob would spark the birth of great theatrical literature".<ref name="picthist">{{cite book |last=Settel |first=Irving |date=1983 |title=A Pictorial History of Television |edition=Second |url=https://archive.org/details/pictorialhistory0000sett_h8s0/page/97/mode/1up |location=New York |publisher=Frederick Ungar Publishing Co |isbn=0804458928 |page=97}}</ref> A different story and a different set of characters were presented in each episode. Very rarely, the stories were split into several episodes, like the 1955 ''Mr. Lincoln'', from the [[Omnibus (U.S. TV series)|Omnibus]] series, which was presented in two parts, or the 1959 adaptation of ''[[For Whom the Bell Tolls]]''<ref>{{cite web|url=https://archive.org/details/SCV31|title=For Whom the Bell Tolls (part 1)|access-date=30 August 2018|year=1959}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://archive.org/details/SCV41|title=For Whom the Bell Tolls (part 2)|access-date=30 August 2018|year=1959}}</ref> from the ''[[Playhouse 90]]'' series, which was initially planned by the director [[John Frankenheimer]] to consist of three parts but was ultimately broadcast in two 90-minute installments.<ref>{{cite web |website=Television Academy Interviews |url=https://interviews.televisionacademy.com/shows/playhouse-90-whom-bell-tolls |title=Playhouse 90: "For Whom the Bell Tolls" |last=Frankenheimer |first=John |date=23 October 2017 }}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=http://wcftr.commarts.wisc.edu/exhibits/golden-age-television/playhouse-90-and-end-golden-age/producing-playhouse-90-whom-bell|title=Producing Playhouse 90: 'For Whom the Bell Tolls' |publisher= The Wisconsin Center for Film and Theater Research|access-date=30 August 2018}}</ref> The high price and technical difficulties of staging a new play every week, which would cost as much as—or more than—an episode of a filmed television series, led to the demise of anthology programming by the end of the 1950s.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://wcftr.commarts.wisc.edu/exhibits/golden-age-television/playhouse-90-and-end-golden-age|title=Playhouse 90 and the end of the Golden Age |publisher= The Wisconsin Center for Film and Theater Research|access-date=30 August 2018}}</ref> The void was filled with less expensive series like ''[[Gunsmoke]]'' or ''[[Wagon Train]]'', which featured the same characters every week and had higher potential for lucrative rebroadcast and syndication rights. It was the American success in 1969–1970 of the British 26-episode serial ''The Forsyte Saga'' that made TV executives realize that finite, multi-episode stories based on novels could be popular and could provide a boost to weekly viewing figures.<ref name="MBC" /><ref name="Wheen" />

According to the ''[[Oxford English Dictionary]]'', the earliest known use of the word "miniseries" was in 1963, when it was employed (in hyphenated form) by the [[Connellsville, Pennsylvania|Connellsville]] ''[[Pittsburgh Tribune-Review|Daily Courier]]'' to describe a limited, five-week run of the [[CBS]] music show ''[[Your Hit Parade]]''.<ref>{{cite web |author=<!--Not stated--> |date=2002 |title=miniseries |url=https://www.oed.com/dictionary/miniseries_n?tab=factsheet#36994693 |url-access=subscription |website=[[Oxford English Dictionary|OED]] |access-date=18 February 2025}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |author=<!--Not stated--> |date=28 July 1963 |title=TV |url=https://www.newspapers.com/image/41902985/ |url-access=subscription |newspaper=[[Pittsburgh Tribune-Review|The Daily Courier]] |location=Connellsville, PA |page=1 |via=[[Newspapers.com]]}}</ref> ''[[The Blue Knight (film)|The Blue Knight]]'', a four-hour, made-for-television movie broadcast in one-hour segments over four nights in November 1973, has elsewhere been credited as the first miniseries on American television.<ref>{{cite book |last=Burlingame |first=Jon |date=1996 |title=TV's Biggest Hits: The Story of Television Themes from "Dragnet" to "Friends" |location=New York |publisher=Schirmer Books |isbn=0028703243 |page=267}}</ref> The miniseries form continued in earnest in the spring of 1974, with the [[Canadian Broadcasting Corporation]]'s eight-part serial ''[[The National Dream (miniseries)|The National Dream]]'', based on [[Pierre Berton]]'s nonfiction book of the same name about the building of the [[Canadian Pacific Railway]], and [[American Broadcasting Company|ABC]]'s two-part ''[[QB VII (miniseries)|QB VII]]'', based on the [[QB VII|novel of the same name]] by [[Leon Uris]]. Following these initial forays, broadcasters used miniseries to bring other books to the screen.{{citation needed|date=October 2021}}

''[[Rich Man, Poor Man (miniseries)|Rich Man, Poor Man]]'', based on the [[Rich Man, Poor Man (novel)|eponymous novel]] by [[Irwin Shaw]], was broadcast in 12 one-hour episodes in 1976 by ABC. It popularized the miniseries format and started a decade-long golden age of television miniseries versions of popular books featuring stars above television class.<ref name="Minihistory" /> [[Alex Haley]]'s ''[[Roots (1977 miniseries)|Roots]]'' in 1977 can fairly be called the first [[Blockbuster (entertainment)|blockbuster]] success of the format. Its success in the USA was partly due to its schedule: the 12-hour duration was split into eight episodes broadcast on consecutive nights, resulting in a finale with a 71 percent share of the audience and 130 million viewers, which at the time was the highest-rated TV program ever. ''[[TV Guide]]'' (11–17 April 1987) called 1977's ''[[Jesus of Nazareth (miniseries)|Jesus of Nazareth]]'' "the best miniseries of all time" and "unparalleled television". ''[[North and South (miniseries)|North and South]]'', the 1985 adaptation of a [[North and South (trilogy)|1982 novel]] by [[John Jakes]], remains one of the ten highest-rated miniseries in TV history.<ref name="TS 2007-08">{{cite web|url=http://www.tribstar.com/news/lifestyles/john-jakes-journey-to-new-york-times-bestseller-list-included/article_8a3877e1-8e78-593d-9de8-b878d396254a.html |title=John Jakes' journey to ''New York Times'' bestseller list included boyhood years in Terre Haute|last=Bennett|first=Mark|date=11 August 2007|work=[[Tribune-Star]]}}</ref><ref name="P&C 2002">{{cite web|url=http://www.depauw.edu/news/index.asp?id=11968 |title=Bestselling novelist penning saga of fictional Charleston family|last=Thompson|first=Bill |date=18 March 2002|work=[[The Post and Courier]]|publisher=Excerpted at DePauw.edu|access-date= 7 January 2010}}</ref><ref name="JJCC">{{cite book|last=Jones|first=Mary Ellen|title=John Jakes: A Critical Companion|publisher=[[Greenwood Publishing Group|Greenwood Press]]|date=30 November 1996 |page= 3|chapter=The People's Author: The Life of John Jakes|isbn=0-313-29530-1 |chapter-url= https://books.google.com/books?id=2pzmvCXfuoAC&q=highest+rated+miniseries+jakes&pg=PA3 |access-date=7 January 2010}}</ref>

===Japan===
Japanese serialized television production can be traced back to the ''Sunday Diary of My Home'', which was aired by [[Nippon Television|NTV]] in 1953 and consisted of 25 half-hour episodes. This "home drama" focused on generational differences and the contradictions of being a loving family in a confined space, outlining a style of drama that lives on to this day. In the same year, NHK tried its own variation of the home drama format in ''Ups and Downs Toward Happiness'', which comprised 13 episodes. Its protagonists, a formerly wealthy family fallen on hard times, is forced to struggle for its own existence.<ref name="dorama-encyclopedia">{{cite book |last1=Clemens |first1=Jonathan |last2=Tamamuro |first2=Motoko |title=The Dorama Encyclopedia: A Guide to Japanese TV Drama Since 1953 |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=z7I-VyCfZdQC&pg=xiii |access-date=22 September 2017 |year=2003 |publisher=Asahi TV |isbn=978-1-880656-81-5 |page=XIII }}</ref> Since then, [[Japanese television drama]], also called {{nihongo|'''dorama'''|ドラマ}}, became a staple of [[Television in Japan|Japanese television]].{{Citation needed|date=March 2026}}

===South Korea===
South Korea began to broadcast [[Korean drama|television series]] ({{Korean|hangul=드라마}}, [[Hangul]] transcription of "drama") in the 1960s. Since then, the shows have become popular worldwide, partially due to the spread of the [[Korean Wave]], with streaming services that offer multiple-language subtitles.{{Citation needed|date=March 2026}}

Korean dramas are usually helmed by one director and written by one screenwriter, thus having a distinct directing style and language, unlike American television series, where often several directors and writers work together.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://english.chosun.com/w21data/html/news/200701/200701080003.html |title=Korean Vs. U.S. Soaps |access-date=19 December 2011 |date=8 January 2007 |publisher=The Chosun Ilbo |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20070109161640/http://english.chosun.com/w21data/html/news/200701/200701080003.html |archive-date= 9 January 2007 |last=Chosun Ilbo}}</ref>

===Soviet Union/Russia===
While the [[Soviet Union]] was among the first European countries to resume television broadcast after the [[Second World War]], early Soviet television did not indulge its viewers with a variety of programming. News, sports, concerts, and movies were the main staples during the 1950s. With state control over television production and broadcast, television was intended not merely for entertainment but also as the means of education and propaganda. Soap operas, quiz shows and games were considered too lowbrow.<ref name="sovtelefilm">{{cite web| url = http://2011.russiancinema.ru/index.php?dept_id=15&e_dept_id=6&text_element_id=36| title = Телевизионное кино в СССР| website = russiancinema.ru| language = ru-RU| trans-title=Television cinema in the USSR |access-date = 22 September 2017| archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20170223064308/http://2011.russiancinema.ru/index.php?dept_id=15&e_dept_id=6&text_element_id=36| archive-date = 23 February 2017| url-status = dead}}</ref>

In the beginning of the 1960s, television was expanding rapidly. The increase in the number of channels and the duration of daily broadcasts caused a shortage of content deemed suitable for broadcast. This led to the production of television films, in particular ''multiple-episode television films'' ({{langx|ru|многосерийный телевизионный фильм}})—the official Soviet moniker for miniseries.<ref name="sovtelefilm" />

The 1965 four-episode ''[[:ru:Вызываем огонь на себя (фильм)|We Call Fire on Ourselves]]'',<ref>{{cite web| url = https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0056793/| title = Vyzyvaem ogon na sebya (Calling for fire, danger close)| website = imdb.com| access-date = 22 September 2017}}</ref> a WWII [[period drama]], is considered the first Soviet miniseries. Other popular miniseries of the Soviet era include the 1973 ''[[Seventeen Moments of Spring]]''; ''[[The Days of the Turbins (1976 film)|Days of the Turbins]]'' (1976)—an adaptation of the [[The Days of the Turbins|play of the same name]] by [[Mikhail Bulgakov]], about the fate of intelligentsia during the [[October Revolution]]; ''[[The Twelve Chairs (1976 film)|The Twelve Chairs]]'' (1976)—an adaptation of the [[The Twelve Chairs|satirical novel of the same name]] by [[Ilf and Petrov]]; ''[[The Meeting Place Cannot Be Changed]]'' (1979); ''[[Little Tragedies (1979 film)|Little Tragedies]]'' (1979)—a collection of short theatrical plays based on works by [[Alexander Pushkin]]; ''[[The Suicide Club, or the Adventures of a Titled Person]]'' (1981) about the adventures of Prince Florizel, a character from [[Robert Louis Stevenson]]'s ''[[The Suicide Club (short story collection)|Suicide Club]]'' stories; ''[[Dead Souls (miniseries)|Dead Souls]]'' (1984,)—an adaptation of the [[Dead Souls|novel of that name]] by [[Nikolai Gogol]]; and ''[[TASS Is Authorized to Declare...]]'' (1984).

Numerous miniseries were produced for children in the 1970s–1980s. Among them are ''[[The Adventures of Buratino (1976 film)|The Adventures of Buratino]]'' (1976)—an adaptation of ''[[The Golden Key, or the Adventures of Buratino]]'' by [[Aleksey Nikolayevich Tolstoy|Alexey Tolstoy]], which in turn is a retelling of ''[[The Adventures of Pinocchio]]'' by [[Carlo Collodi]]; ''[[The Adventures of the Elektronic]]'' (1979); and ''[[Guest from the Future]]'' (1985).

After the dissolution of the Soviet Union in 1991, Russian television experienced a period of privatization and liberalization. Starting from the 2000s, a resurgence of book adaptations took place, such as ''[[The Idiot (TV series)|The Idiot]]'' (2003)—an adaptation of the [[The Idiot|novel]] by [[Fyodor Dostoyevsky]], and ''[[The Master and Margarita (miniseries)|The Master and Margarita]]'' (2005)—an adaptation of the [[The Master and Margarita|novel]] by Mikhail Bulgakov.

===Brazil===
In Brazil, the [[Rede Globo]] television network commenced the production of miniseries with the transmission of ''Lampião e Maria Bonita'',<ref>{{cite web |last=Zanetti |first=Laysa |date=29 December 2014 |title=Rede Globo irá reexibir séries em formato condensado: Saiba quais |url=http://www.adorocinema.com/noticias/series/noticia-111016/ |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20250713143726/http://www.adorocinema.com/noticias/series/noticia-111016/ |archive-date=13 July 2025 |access-date=16 January 2018 |website=[[AdoroCinema]] |language=pt-BR |trans-title=Rede Globo will re-air series in condensed format: Find out which ones.}}</ref> written by [[Aguinaldo Silva]] and [[Doc Comparato]] and directed by Paulo Afonso Grisolli, and broadcast in 1982 in eight episodes.<ref>{{cite web| url = http://teledramaturgia.com.br/lampiao-e-maria-bonita/| title = Lampião e Maria Bonita – Sinopse| language = pt-BR
| access-date = 16 January 2018 |trans-title=Lampião e Maria Bonita – synopsis}}</ref><ref>{{cite web| url = http://teledramaturgia.com.br/minisseries/| title = Minisséries| language = pt-BR| access-date = 16 January 2018}}</ref>

===Australia===
The first locally produced miniseries in Australia was ''[[Against the Wind (miniseries)|Against the Wind]]'', which aired in 1978.<ref>{{Citation |last=Cunningham |first=Stuart |chapter=Textual Innovation in the Australian Historical Mini-Series |date=28 July 2020 |chapter-url=http://dx.doi.org/10.4324/9781003114949-4 |title=Australian Television |pages=39–51 |access-date=15 March 2023 |publisher=Routledge |doi=10.4324/9781003114949-4 |isbn=978-1-003-11494-9|s2cid=225356205 |chapter-url-access=subscription }}</ref> Over one hundred miniseries were produced in Australia over the next decade.<ref name=":1">{{Cite book |first=Stuart |last=Cunningham |title=Contemporary Australian television |date=1994 |publisher=N.S.W. University Press |isbn=0-86840-397-0 |oclc=986689762}}</ref> Historical dramas were particularly popular with Australian audiences during this period.{{Citation needed|date=March 2026}} Between 1984 and 1987, 27 out of a total of 34 Australian-made miniseries had historical themes.<ref>{{Cite book |author=Paterson, Richard Smith, Anthony |title=Television: an international history |date=2005 |publisher=Oxford Univ. Press |isbn=0-19-815928-5 |oclc=255698381}}</ref> Some notable examples included ''[[The Dismissal (miniseries)|The Dismissal]]'', ''[[Bodyline (miniseries)|Bodyline]]'', ''[[Eureka Stockade (miniseries)|Eureka Stockade]]'', ''[[The Cowra Breakout (miniseries)|The Cowra Breakout]]'', ''[[Vietnam (miniseries)|Vietnam]]'', and ''[[Brides of Christ]]''. The narratives of these miniseries often followed one or two fictionalized individuals in the context of actual historical events and situations.<ref name=":2">{{Citation |last=Moran |first=Albert |chapter=Three Stages of Australian Television |date=28 July 2020 |chapter-url=http://dx.doi.org/10.4324/9781003114949-1 |title=Australian Television |pages=1–14 |access-date=15 March 2023 |publisher=Routledge |doi=10.4324/9781003114949-1 |isbn=978-1-003-11494-9|s2cid=225485835 |chapter-url-access=subscription }}</ref> Literary adaptations were also popular, with notable examples including ''[[A Town Like Alice (miniseries)|A Town Like Alice]]'', ''[[A Fortunate Life#Miniseries|A Fortunate Life]]'', ''[[The Harp in the South#1986 miniseries|The Harp in the South]]'', and ''[[Come in Spinner (miniseries)|Come in Spinner]]''.

The 1983 miniseries ''[[Return to Eden]]'' was Australia's most successful to date, with over 300 million viewers around the world. It has been described as "the best Australian example of the melodramatic miniseries".<ref>{{Cite book |first=Andrew |last=Mercado |title=Super Aussie soaps: behind the scenes of Australia's best loved TV shows |date=2006 |publisher=Vision Australia Information Library Service |oclc=1122566950}}</ref><ref>{{Cite book |last=Humphries |first=Scott |title=The Age of Melodramatic Miniseries: When Glamour Ruled on Television, 1980–1995 |publisher=McFarland and Company |year=2023 |isbn=9781476691626 |location=Jefferson, North Carolina |page=151}}</ref>

The number of Australian-made miniseries declined in the 1990s, and many of those that were produced had more of an "international" focus, often starring American or British actors in leading roles and/or being filmed outside of Australia.<ref name=":1" /> Some notable examples included ''[[The Last Frontier (miniseries)|The Last Frontier]]'', ''[[Which Way Home (miniseries)|Which Way Home]]'', ''[[A Dangerous Life]]'', ''[[Bangkok Hilton]]'', and ''[[Dadah Is Death]]''.

===Egypt===
In [[Egypt]], the 1980s and 1990s was the golden age of television miniseries, attracting millions of viewers. For example, ''[[The Family of Mr Shalash]]'', starring [[Salah Zulfikar]], was the highest-rated at the time.<ref>{{cite web|last=Kijamii|title=15 Timeless Egyptian Series You Should Watch Over and Over Again {{!}} NileFM {{!}} Egypt's #1 for Hit Music|url=https://nilefm.com/tv-shows/article/335/15-egyptian-timeless-series-you-should-watch-over-and-over-again|access-date=7 January 2022|website=nilefm.com}}</ref>

==Popularity==
The 1983, American, eighteen-hour miniseries ''[[The Winds of War (miniseries)|The Winds of War]]'' was a ratings success, with 140 million viewers for all or part of the show, making it the most-watched miniseries up to that time.<ref name=":0">{{Cite news|url=https://www.newspapers.com/clip/41095404/winds_of_war_ratings/|title='Winds of War' Takes Ratings by Storm|last=Lewis|first=Dan|date=16 February 1983|work=The Record|access-date=26 December 2019|via=[[Newspapers.com]]|page=53}}</ref> Its 1988 sequel, ''[[War and Remembrance (miniseries)|War and Remembrance]]'', won several [[Emmy Awards|Emmys]] and [[Golden Globes]] and was considered by some critics the ultimate epic miniseries on American television.<ref name="Minihistory">{{cite book|url=https://books.google.com/books?id=OtQ8DuHHouQC&q=miniseries+decline&pg=PA89|title=Epic Television Miniseries: A Critical History|first1=John|last1=De Vito|first2=Frank|last2=Tropea|date=8 March 2010|publisher=McFarland |isbn=9780786457335}}</ref> However, it also signalled the start of the format's decline, as the $105&nbsp;million production was a major ratings flop; the advent of [[VCR]] and [[cable television]] options was responsible for the decrease of length and ratings of most miniseries that continued into the mid-1990s.<ref name="Minihistory" /> By 1996, [[Gulliver's Travels (miniseries)|the highest-rated miniseries of the winter season]] garnered a rating of 19, less than the average of 22 for [[ER (TV series)|that same season's top-rated regular series]].<ref name="Minihistory" />

In the 21st century, the format made a comeback on cable television and became popular on [[Streaming television|streaming]] services. [[History (U.S. TV channel)|History]], for example, has had some of its greatest successes with miniseries such as ''[[America: The Story of Us]]'', ''[[Hatfields & McCoys (miniseries)|Hatfields & McCoys]]'', and ''[[The Bible (miniseries)|The Bible]]''.{{citation needed|date=September 2017}} ''[[Political Animals (miniseries)|Political Animals]]'' by [[USA Network]] was honored with a [[Critics' Choice Television Award for Most Exciting New Series]],<ref>{{cite web|title=Critics' Choice TV Awards: 'Homeland', 'Community' & 'Sherlock' Double Winners |url=https://deadline.com/2012/06/critics-choice-television-awards-2012-winners-288293/ |website=deadline.com |date=18 June 2012 |access-date=21 March 2026}}</ref> while HBO's ''[[Big Little Lies (miniseries)|Big Little Lies]]'' (which was eventually renewed for a second season) won eight Emmy awards.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.emmys.com/shows/big-little-lies|title=Television Academy: Big Little Lies|access-date=22 September 2017}}</ref>

To designate one-season shows that are not intended for being renewal, the broadcast and television industries came up with terms like "limited series" or "event series". These terms also apply to multi-season anthology series, such as ''[[American Horror Story]]'', ''[[Fargo (TV series)|Fargo]]'', and ''[[True Detective]]''. This makes the self-contained season longer than a miniseries but shorter than the entire run of the multi-season series. This terminology became relevant for the purpose of [[Primetime Emmy Award for Outstanding Limited Series|categorization]] of programs for [[Primetime Emmy Award|industry awards]].<ref name="TVGuide">Schneider, Michael (9 March 2015). "Inside the Emmys' New Rules". ''[[TV Guide]]''. pp 8–9.</ref>

Several television executives interviewed by ''[[The Hollywood Reporter]]'' stated that the term "miniseries" has negative connotations to the public, having become associated with [[melodrama]]-heavy works that were commonly produced under the format, while "limited series" or "event series" demand higher respect.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.hollywoodreporter.com/news/heroes-24-whats-difference-between-683563|title=''Heroes'', ''24'': What's the Difference Between a 'Miniseries,' 'Limited' or 'Event' Series?|author=Lacey Rose and Lesley Goldberg|date=28 February 2014|work=[[The Hollywood Reporter]]|access-date=25 February 2015}}</ref> The parody miniseries ''[[The Spoils of Babylon]]'' lampooned many of the negative stereotypes of miniseries.<ref name="production">{{cite web|last=Locker|first=Melissa|title=IFC's "The Spoils of Babylon" to feature Jessica Alba, Will Ferrell, Toby Maguire, Kristen Wiig, Tim Robbins and more|url=http://www.ifc.com/fix/2013/06/ifcs-the-spoils-of-babylon-to-feature-jessica-alba-will-ferrell-toby-maguire-kristen-wiig-tim-robbins-and-more|publisher=[[IFC (U.S. TV network)|IFC]]|date=3 June 2013|access-date=3 June 2013}}</ref>

In the 21st century, two miniseries have had significant impact on [[pop culture]] and are often named the two best shows ever made: ''[[Band of Brothers (miniseries)|Band of Brothers]]'', released in 2001, and ''[[Chernobyl (miniseries)|Chernobyl]]'', which came out in 2019. When the final episode of ''Chernobyl'' aired, it was already the highest-rated show in [[IMDb]] history.<ref>{{cite web|last=Spangler|first=Todd|date=5 June 2019|title=HBO's 'Chernobyl' Is Now the Top-Rated TV Show on IMDb|url=https://variety.com/2019/digital/news/chernobyl-top-rated-tv-show-all-time-1203233833/|access-date=22 April 2021|website=Variety}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|last=Tassi|first=Paul|title='Chernobyl' Ends Its HBO Run as the Highest Audience Rated TV Series in History|url=https://www.forbes.com/sites/paultassi/2019/06/04/chernobyl-ends-its-hbo-run-as-the-highest-audience-rated-tv-series-in-history/|access-date=22 April 2021|website=Forbes}}</ref>

The miniseries as a format has become more popular than ever before.<ref name="Davies 2020" /><ref>{{cite web | title=The Rise and Rise of the Mini-Series |date=24 August 2021| website=[[Australian Centre for the Moving Image]] | url=http://www.acmi.net.au/whats-on/meet-the-creators-aftrs-acmi/the-rise-and-rise-of-the-mini-series/ | access-date=25 October 2021}}</ref>
